>   
> It has to be this way.

>

>   
> Neither would be incomplete without the other, but both would find their greatest joy in re-uniting with their counterpart.

>   
> The Bond  
>   
>

This protective instinct isn't born from recognizing a weaker or incomplete being, but from recognizing another complete version of himself, worthy of the ultimate protection and care.

> **@vicky-n**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=UgwXYN2xdYDndsZ99vR4AaABAg.ANcY4MI3TUyAPZxa9k_dOA)  
>
> ⁠​⁠​⁠@coeburn9588does social media ever have substance? When you go to sleep, what do you remember about the things you watched/scrolled about? For me it’s nothing. Maybe 2 or three posts. But for the most part, nothing I scroll about on social media sticks with me if there’s not a theme for the videos. If I scroll for 30 min about one theme then chances are I remember it & the knowledge better. That’s how our brains work, with repetition or information programming as taught in this class.   
>   
> Again , when I scroll for 1 hour or more unintentionally or without a theme/topic, i retain very little.   
>   
> So what I suggest is, when you scroll, scroll in “bulk” meaning Scroll with an intention or topic in mind. So for example if you come across a reel or tiktok about Egypt idk then follow the thread of thought/the thread of the topic. And maybe that journey takes you to learning what the city looks like or what the clothes people wear are or etc. usually this works better tho w a popular topic tho.    
>   
> Hopefully by then, your curiosity will be activated and you’ll be able to detach from short form content. You’ll be able to scale up to movies and books or magazines and podcasts or even lectures and essays. Academic essays really help me tbh when I can’t find video essays on a topic.   
>   
> And if you can develop the learning habit but still scroll by habit, try stopping when you get an ad. This is what I do and it helps cut the habit real fast. I get in maybe 5 posts and then get an ad lol.   
> If you find that having your phone be so accessible is the issue, then put your phone out of immediate sight say across the room or in a drawer and only check it every hour or so. You could also leave the ringer on if you’re worried about missing any important calls. It can be in reach just not immediate reach yk?   
> And if possible, bring up what you scroll to retain your knowledge in conversation whether that be w a friend or with yourself via Word or Docs.   
>   
> Btw the movies/media you watch don’t even have to be academic. They could be fictional and shi; the purpose is just to be reminded of the actual theme in concept.   
> So say my interest is dinosaurs, i could watch Jurassic park to retain the concept of dinosaurs over the facts. Yk keep the train of thought/inner convo about dinosaurs going.   
> I know common advice is to go cold turkey but, at least for me, that didn’t work in the long run.   
>   
> Maybe the way to remember the is:  
> 1. Scroll / Find Hook  
> 2. Explore / Search more   
> 3: Report   
>   
> Try this if you’d like and feel free to lmk how it goes 🙏

I really respect this professor and appreciate his insight, but I have to disagree with his stance on what used to be called Multiple Personality Disorder, now known as Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID). People with DID absolutely can experience happiness and build loving, long-lasting relationships. DID is a trauma response, not a limitation on a person’s capacity to love or live fully. Many survivors of severe or ritualistic abuse have DID and still go on to create meaningful, joyful lives. It’s important to challenge the stigma and recognize the strength and resilience of people living with DID.

> I wanted to take the time to discern on this comment because in the context of the video you instill a negative connotation of Freemasonry - when in fact it is akin to the Order of the White Lotus in Avatar the Last Airbender.  To quote :"The Order of the White Lotus, also known simply as the White Lotus, is an ancient and formerly secret society that transcends the boundaries of the four nations, seeking philosophy, beauty, and truth. They are devoted to sharing ancient knowledge across national and political divides."  
>   
> In Freemasonry, Brotherly Love is one of the three key tenets, along with Relief and Truth, emphasizing universal love, kindness, tolerance, and mutual respect among all people. It teaches Masons to treat the entire human race as one family, regardless of wealth or status, providing support and aid to others. This esoterically is akin to love thy neighbor in the bible and the Professor's quick introduction at @32:00. Its easy to love your family, friends, significant other, ect. But to love those that you have never met, or the man beating his wife, or the murderer. That is the secret of the philosophers stone, the monad, the redemption arc of Jesus Christ dying on the cross for our sins. It takes masters years to train this -  to transform the soul from lead to gold in this aspect of love. It is the Gnostic redemption myth of Sophia's fall into the abyss. Sophia as the divine spark transformed through the Logos of the Monad.   
>   
> To quote Plato's Symposium : "'These are the lesser mysteries of love, into which even you, Socrates, may enter; to the greater and more hidden ones which are the crown of these, and to which, if you pursue them in a right spirit, they will lead, I know not whether you will be able to attain. But I will do my utmost to inform you, and do you follow if you can. For he who would proceed aright in this matter should begin in youth to visit beautiful forms; and first, if he be guided by his instructor aright, to love one such form only—out of that he should create fair thoughts; and soon he will of himself perceive that the beauty of one form is akin to the beauty of another; and then if beauty of form in general is his pursuit, how foolish would he be not to recognize that the beauty in every form is and the same! And when he perceives this he will abate his violent love of the one, which he will despise and deem a small thing, and will become a lover of all beautiful forms; in the next stage he will consider that the beauty of the mind is more honorable than the beauty of the outward form."  
>   
> To quote Charles Vail in The Ancient Mysteries and Modern Masonry : "For some centuries before the Christian era the Essenean communities dwelt on the shores of the Dead Sea. Their chief characteristic was the doctrine of love — love of God, love of virtue, and love of human kind...   
>   
> The Essenes had their degrees and Initiations, in which we find the identity of teaching between Essenism and Christianity is most marked. Mr. Mead sums up these points of contact somewhat as follows : Converts were required to sell their possessions and give to the poor, for the laying up of treasure was regarded as injurious to a spiritual life. Not only did the Essenes despise riches, but they lived a life of self-imposed poverty. Love of the brotherhood and of one's neighbor was the soul of Essene life, and the basis of all action; and this characteristic of their discipline called forth universal admiration. The members lived together as a family, had all things in common, and pointed a steward to manage the common purse. When traveling they would lodge with brethren whom they had never seen before, as though with the oldest and most intimate friends; and thus they took nothing with them when they went on a journey. All members were on the same level, and the authority of one over another was forbidden; nevertheless mutual service was mutually enjoined. They were also great lovers of peace, and so refused to take arms or manufacture warlike weapons; moreover they proscribed slavery. The ultimate aim of the Essenes was to be meek and lowly in spirit, to mortify all sinful lusts, to be pure in heart, to hate evil but reclaim the evil doer, and to be merciful to all men and moreover, their yea was to be yea, and their nay, nay. They were devoted to the curing of the sick and the healing of both body and soul, regarding the power to perform miraculous cures and cast out evil spirits as the highest stage of discipline. In brief, they strove to be so pure as to become temples of the Holy Spirit, and thus seers and prophets. To these inner communities were attached outer circles of pupils living in the world, and found in all the main centres of the Diaspora."   
>   
> - A supplemental book to the above is Mystic Christianity; Or, The Inner Teachings of the Master by Yogi Ramacharaka:   
>   
> "When Jesus reached his native land, after the years of travel in India, Persia and Egypt, he is believed to have spent at least one year among the various lodges and retreats of the Essenes. By reference to the first lesson of this series you will see who and what was this great mystic organization--the Essenic Brotherhood. While resting and studying in their retreats His attention was diverted to the work of Johannen--John the Baptist--and He saw there an opening wedge for the great work that He felt called
  
> upon to do among His own people."  
>   
> The Essenes included Zadokite priests who were considered the legitimate, exiled remnant of the Zadokite Priesthood after it was seen as corrupted by the Sadducees and the Hellenistic rulers of the time. This group of priests, along with other pious individuals, formed the Essene sect, a monastic-like community that established its own strict rules and practices, including their own forms of worship separate from the Temple in Jerusalem. Jesus was killed because Roman authorities viewed him as a political threat for claiming to be the "King of the Jews" and an insurrectionist, a claim that could incite rebellion. While religious leaders found his teachings on God and his authority offensive, the decisive factor in his crucifixion was his challenge to Roman rule, which was a capital offense in Roman-occupied Palestine.

> It’s completely true that political violence is on the rise in America, and it’s in line with the second American civil war thought experiment from the geopolitics class. However, as someone else just pointed out, this wasn’t even the first major political assasination this year, and the last one was literally a lifelong Republican activist killing Minnesota lawmakers to change the balance of power in the state legislature. To act like this is somehow more special or impactful shows an extreme bias. I will say I didn’t agree with Charlie Kirk on a single thing but I also don’t believe any true leftist would carry out the murder of another human being just because he is a skilled propagandist for his side. Whoever did this, IMO, is either a batshit crazy military type, or it was one of the nazi accelerationists that have been openly feuding with him lately. All political violence should be denounced, the people in this thread celebrating it suck, and the people saying stuff like “biggest assasination since Franz Ferdinand” have no sense of history and have been consuming a selective media diet that filters out all the inconvenient shit for them. Charlie Kirk had horrible opinions from my point of view, and his popularity was unfathomable to me, but that was while he was engaging in what he considered his work. He was also a father of two young children, and a husband, just 31 years old. Much as I disagreed with his politics, if you can’t recognize that for the human tragedy it is, and if you can’t see the road this is leading us down, your inhumanity is showing. Exact same goes for those that downplay or don’t even know about the Minnesota assassinations. This shit should be off limits and denounced by everyone, otherwise it just becomes a never-ending revenge loop, which is frankly what this administration seems to have been trying to find and roll with.   
>   
> I will say that shot was pretty obviously taken by someone who is an expert marksman, and I don’t know many leftists that practice with sniper rifles. In other words I don’t think Kirk’s fans, if they really want justice, should settle for the explanation that suits their worldview the most. That said, my heart goes out to anyone that feels affected by this tragic event, whether it’s fans of his or people like me who abhor violence and still see our political opponents as human beings who simply have a different worldview and beliefs than we do.

My brother, a resident doctor at the time, was begging me to reconsider antidepressants but I didn't have the kind of awareness that's needed to understand his point and see through these systems designed to manipulate us. I went through 6 different psychiatrists and then finally found one who, on multiple tries, tapered me off of those meds and put me in intensive talk therapy. She also pointed me in the direction of self-reflection and self-parenting to unlearn/learn better ways. Before treatment, I had big, wild ideas and was way too inquisitive by nature, which, for many reasons, is never encouraged (especially not in young women where I come from). When on medication, and even years after stopping, I realised I've just become overall indifferent and numb. Now, in my 30s, I actively try to tap into my inquisitiveness, and I want to question everything again. And that's exactly what they don't want us to do.

>
> An NDE (Near-Death Experience) is generally classified as a profound psychological event that occurs when someone comes close to death or is in a situation where death seems imminent, but survives. It’s not just one symptom—it’s a cluster of phenomena that many people report across cultures and circumstances.  
>   
> Researchers typically classify an NDE using the following core features (often measured by the Greyson NDE Scale):  
>   
> 1. Context  
> 	•	Happens during life-threatening events such as cardiac arrest, severe trauma, drowning, anesthesia complications, or intense psychological stress.  
> 	•	Can also occur in situations where death is not medically imminent but is strongly perceived as such.  
>   
> 2. Common Characteristics  
> 	•	Out-of-body experiences (OBEs): Feeling separated from one’s body, sometimes viewing it from above.  
> 	•	Altered time perception: Time may feel sped up, slowed down, or nonexistent.  
> 	•	Intense emotions: Feelings of peace, love, or euphoria; sometimes fear or distress in rare cases.  
> 	•	Tunnel experiences: Moving through a tunnel toward a light or “other side.”  
> 	•	Encounters: Seeing deceased relatives, spiritual beings, or life reviews.

### **@DIYDSP** | 👍 38 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=UgzMsunerqH_lYJHcCV4AaABAg)

Careful everyone: this is highly overgeneralizing and meant to be shocking.  

A few things to unpack:

Unpredictability. Some leaders do cultivate mystique or surprise to keep rivals off balance (Napoleon, Nixon, Trump, even FDR in his maneuvering). But many great leaders are valued precisely for being predictable—Lincoln, Mandela, Washington—because people could trust their word.

High stress tolerance. True that resilience under pressure is common among leaders. But that doesn’t mean they were stress-immune. Churchill famously had bouts of depression; Lincoln lost sleep over war casualties. What mattered is that they functioned despite stress.

Lack of empathy. This one’s tricky. Some very effective authoritarian or war leaders were ruthless and detached (Stalin, Mao, Caesar). But others—think of Gandhi, Mandela, even Roosevelt—built their strength on empathy and connection. In fact, modern research on leadership in business and politics shows empathy is a core predictor of influence and loyalty.

“Dissociation.” That’s the professor trying to wrap it in one concept, but it’s a stretch. Dissociation in psychology means detachment from one’s self or environment—it’s not a standard leadership theory term. He’s co-opting it to mean “they don’t feel what others feel,” but that’s not historically universal.

### **@Jest-4-Fun**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=Ugz_3bbwPKobK_8KbOp4AaABAg)

Summarize the video
Professor Jiang's lecture, "The Psychology of Evil," explores how ancient mind control techniques, originating in Egypt, are still used today (0:01-0:09). He draws parallels between the programming of Egyptian pharaohs and modern methods of social control, including the War on Terror and social media.

The lecture covers:

Historical context of mind control (3:07-5:51): Professor Jiang discusses the social control mechanisms of early mass societies, including China's bureaucracy, the Indus Valley Civilization's religion, Mesopotamia's warfare, and Egypt's deified Pharaoh.
Characteristics of leaders and dissociation (6:10-12:08): The professor argues that effective leaders possess unpredictability, high stress tolerance, and a lack of empathy—qualities he collectively terms "dissociation." He links dissociation to multiple personality disorder (9:20).
Egyptian mythology as a programming script (12:44-23:08): Egyptian mythology, with its main characters Ra (virtuous hero), Osiris (passive victim), and Horus (vengeful child), is presented not as a story but as a script for programming the pharaoh. Through rituals, psychedelics, and trauma, priests would induce dissociative states to create different identities in the pharaoh, making him controllable. The use of scents to activate these identities is also explained (22:20).
Modern applications of mind control (32:31-47:25): The lecture connects ancient Egyptian mind control techniques to the "enhanced interrogation" methods used in the War on Terror, particularly at Abu Ghraib prison. He suggests that the psychological theories used were designed to turn prisoners into programmable "human drones" for chaos in the Middle East, leading to the creation of groups like ISIS. He also discusses the CIA's MKUltra program (43:18) and speculates on its modern-day relevance in social media and psychology.
Heaven, hell, and the spirit world (30:21-32:15): A student's question about heaven and hell leads to a discussion on near-death experiences (NDEs), suggesting that those who experience NDEs describe a "life review" where they feel the pain or joy they inflicted on others.
Social media as a tool for social control (48:28-50:16): The professor suggests that social media platforms, though seemingly privately owned, are part of a larger infrastructure (like the internet built by the US military) designed for social control. He reiterates that these are speculations and encourages skepticism.


Why is school so hard?
How do leaders use dissociation?
The video states that leaders use dissociation as a key skill (8:36-8:40). According to the speaker, dissociation means that one's mind leaves the body, allowing them to perceive events as if they are happening to someone else, like an observer in a movie (8:46-9:06).

This leads to three characteristics that the speaker claims are essential for great leaders:

Unpredictability: People cannot predict a leader's behavior, making them a mystery (7:17-7:31, 9:09-9:12).
High stress tolerance: Leaders are not bothered by external negativity or pressure (7:35-7:58, 9:11).
Lack of empathy: Leaders are selfish and do not care what happens to others (8:01-8:10, 9:12).
The speaker further explains that multiple personality disorder is another word for dissociation in this context (9:19-9:27). Great leaders throughout history, such as Donald Trump, are presented as unpredictable because they embody "a hundred different people in a different circumstance" (9:27-9:44). In game theory, winning is associated with being part of multiple powerful secret alliances, which necessitates creating multiple personalities to maintain loyalty without true betrayal (10:30-11:27). Spies are also said to possess this skill of quickly dissociating, which the speaker equates to a lack of empathy and psychopathy (11:29-11:43). The ultimate goal, from the perspective of those controlling leaders, is to program them into a "robot" (24:43-25:21). The speaker emphasizes that dissociation is a crucial characteristic for those in power to survive, as being a "nice person" would lead to their downfall (26:09-26:22).



Why is Egyptian mythology unique?
How does modern psychology brainwash?
What's "predictive history"?
While the video is from a channel called "Predictive History" and the professor's Substack is also "Predictive History," the video itself does not define or explain what "predictive history" means. The content focuses on historical examples of mind control and their potential modern-day parallels.



How did priests gain ancient knowledge?
How does wealth affect leader survival?
What makes evil "secret"?
In the context of the video, "secret" evil refers to the hidden and manipulative techniques used for mind control and social engineering that are not openly acknowledged or understood by the general public.

Here's what makes it "secret" according to the professor:

Hidden mechanisms of control (0:07-0:09): The video introduces mind control as the "secret weapon" of secret societies, implying that its methods and applications are concealed.
Governmental and state secrets (25:46-25:48): The professor states that the ancient Egyptian mind control techniques would have been the "greatest state secret of Egypt," suggesting that such powerful tools are intentionally kept hidden by those in power.
Concealed effectiveness of programs (46:02-46:06): When discussing MKUltra, the professor suggests that its effectiveness was deliberately hidden and that its results have "spread throughout society" without public awareness.
Behind-the-scenes infrastructure (49:24-49:34): In the context of social media, the professor posits that while platforms like Facebook and Twitter are public, the "infrastructure that's being controlled and protected by the US military" operates secretly.


Why are psychologists "screwed up"?
The video claims that psychologists are "screwed up" based on the speaker's observations and assertions (46:39-46:41).

According to the speaker:

Psychologists have a greater propensity to have mental illness than most people (46:30-46:37).
The speaker personally states they have not met anyone who has gotten better psychologically after seeing a psychologist (47:02-47:07), finding this "really weird" compared to other medical professions (47:17-47:2027-47:25).


How is programming maintained?
According to the video, programming is maintained through several mechanisms that create and reinforce specific identities and behaviors:

Trauma and Dissociation (21:08-21:18): The core method involves inducing trauma, which leads to dissociation. This psychological state causes the mind to detach from the body, making the individual feel like an observer of their own experiences. This detachment allows for the programming of different identities.
Ritual and Experience Simulation (18:49-19:22): Rituals, often combined with psychedelics, are used to create simulated experiences that are so vivid the individual believes they are truly happening to them. This "rewrites" their memories and creates new belief systems.
Triggering through Scent (22:20-23:08): In the Egyptian context, different scents were used to activate specific programmed identities and emotions in the Pharaoh, influencing their decisions. The professor suggests this is a general principle, where external circumstances or triggers activate different "identities" within individuals (e.g., student at school, child at home).
Muscle Memory and Subconscious Training (24:35-25:03): The video uses the analogy of a skilled soccer player losing their memory but still being able to play, explaining that intensive training can embed behaviors into muscle memory and the subconscious. This allows for programmed actions to occur even without conscious awareness or memory. For example, a trained killer might act upon a trigger word due to this subconscious programming (25:05-25:15).
Controller Dependency (21:35-22:10): By having a supportive figure (like Isis in the Egyptian mythology) present during traumatic programming, the victim becomes dependent on this figure. This figure, who is a priest in disguise, then becomes the "controller" who can subtly influence the programmed individual, even whispering instructions while they are vulnerable (e.g., sleeping or drugged).

How can we train ourselves to remember how to think clearly in everyday life, when we are tired, stressed, or overwhelmed by routine, so that these concepts actually guide our behaviour rather than remaining just “interesting ideas”?

What, in your view, are the real dangers of having psychopaths or dissociated personalities in positions of power? To me, it sometimes feels as if their main function is to generate fear and anxiety in the population through constant fear‑mongering.

5:12 Mesopotamia did use war to organize their society but the main cause were the irregular floods of the Tigris and Eufrates. Control of the areas near the rivers was paramount for the hegemon in that area and due to large periods of droughts and/or floods (which is a common theme in mesopotamian myths) there were often raiders and warlords vying for control. These in essence water wars caused a lot of refugees, including Ur, where Abraham fled from.
This in STARK contrast to Egypt where the regular ebb and flow of the Nile was so predictable that we can still find ancient hieroglyphs near the Nile with data for when to plant and harvest in accordance with the Nile. This is evident in the how long Egypt has been stable, no civilization has ever reached that longevity. This is also evident in their religion which is mostly pretty peaceful, fair and just, compared to mesopotamian religions.

### **@monsieurgarf4058**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=Ugw6fXX2VAHVnFjZB0J4AaABAg)

For people who get caught up in blindly believing Jiang, although he does have a lot of thought provoking and brilliant perspectives it’s important to question some of the things he says.
1) it’s not as easy as giving someone drugs, showing them how to shoot someone and assigning a trigger word to have them activate as some sort of sleeper agent outside their will power. It would be far more complex than that.
2) Near death experiences aren’t as predictable as he makes seem. Some people report them in situations where they die in hospital or are knocked unconscious/nearly die in a car accident but can be associated with more rationale explanations such as bright lights in a hospital operating room or lights used by emergency response. In addition attributed to hallucinations speculated that your body induces to prevent going into shock. Some claim this is caused by a release of DMT in the body which is also released while sleeping as well as can be consumed as a concentrated drug in various forms.
3) when consuming heavy psychedelics such as DMT, acid, salvia, mushrooms… a lot of THAT is programmed into your experience as well. If you’re told you are seeing god, you will believe it. When I smoked DMT and had a breakthrough experience I understood the chemical impact it was having on my brain and body rather than thinking it was some portal. To me it’s like watching a television screen in first person where you can’t control what’s on television. I used it and kept my eyes open. Basically when I entered the state, everything that was visible before expanded into an infinite number of that object until my vision was completely encompassed by something completely different. Dull grass on the hill expanded into an infinite number of vibrant neon green blades until they became a blurred kaleidoscope, the water I knew that was below me looked like the loading screen of the original couple assassins creed games, the person sitting next to me looked like a frog like creature encased in a dense soap bubble. 5 minutes or so past and everything was back to normal. To experience that and believe it was some doorway to heaven or spirit realm - well, maybe - but more likely just momentary change in your brain chemistry.

### **@azraelchristos321**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=Ugy1pISYzvOC0yMd1Ip4AaABAg)

I want to say this without being banned, because I love this channel. Here is my question though, why doesn't the Professor cover Eastern Civilization's religious beliefs throughout history and primarily focuses on only Western Civilization's religious beliefs throughout history? If the Professor is indeed trying to tell the full truth, then both sides matter very much. There are two or more sides to anything and everything in reality, not just one. 

"Yes, the channel you're watching is **Predictive History** (@PredictiveHistory on YouTube), where **Professor Jiang Xueqin** (often just called Professor Jiang) delivers lectures, primarily to his high school students in Beijing. The video you linked (Secret History #6: The Psychology of Evil) is part of his ongoing "Secret History" series, which explores hidden or alternative interpretations of historical events, mind control, secret societies, power structures, and religious/psychological developments.

His content heavily focuses on **Western civilization's religious and philosophical evolution** — for example:
- Stages of Western religion (mother goddess worship → polytheism → monotheism).
- Origins and manipulations of Judaism (e.g., Persian influences in constructing Jewish identity).
- The creation and spread of Christianity (e.g., Paul's role, Jesus' teachings reinterpreted, the Catholic Church as an empire).
- Zoroastrianism's invention of monotheism (via Zarathustra).
- Broader critiques tying these to modern control mechanisms, geopolitics, and "evil" in history.

He does touch on some non-Western elements, like ancient Egypt (as a source of mind control techniques via priests and mythology), Mesopotamia, the Indus Valley (brief comparisons for social control), and even Islam (Muhammad's revolution and the Islamic Golden Age's contributions to modernity/mathematics). There's also occasional reference to China in his background or predictive geopolitics.

However, the core narrative and detailed deep dives are overwhelmingly on **Western** (Abrahamic, Greco-Roman, European) religious history and its ties to power/evil. The "Secret History" series appears to culminate in topics like "Pax Judaica" and related endgames, staying within that Western-influenced sphere.

He has a separate playlist/course called **"Civilization"** (or "The Story of Civilization"), which covers humanity's broad story from the Ice Age onward, including Muhammad/Islam and potentially wider civilizational arcs. But even there, much of the emphasis (based on available episodes) remains on how these intersect with or contrast to Western developments.

He does **not** seem to have a major shift or dedicated series to Eastern civilizations' religions in the same depth — like detailed explorations of Confucianism, Taoism, Buddhism, Hinduism's philosophical/religious views across history, or Shinto/Indian traditions as primary focuses. Eastern elements appear more as contrasts, brief mentions, or in geopolitical context rather than a comprehensive parallel treatment.

If you're hoping for equivalent coverage of Eastern religions (e.g., how Confucianism or Buddhism shaped worldviews, or their historical evolutions), it hasn't happened prominently yet in the main channels. His style is broad and predictive/speculative, so future lectures could expand, but the pattern so far prioritizes critiquing/explaining Western religious history and its global impacts. You can check his playlists (like "Civilization" or "Secret History") or Substack for updates."

Professor,

I understand the argument you are making. But I think it misses the core issue. IS is not just a “tool.”It is an idea.
And ideas do not die when you kill a leader. They mutate.
They travel. They recruit through meaning, not salaries.

If ISIS were simply an American robot, the math would not add up. No state spends years bleeding money, personnel, and political capital to “fight” what it fully controls.
That is not how power works.

What keeps ISIS alive is not one sponsor. It is ideology plus conditions.

A rigid worldview. A simple story of purity and revenge.
A promise of identity to people who feel humiliated and disposable. And local chaos that gives the story oxygen.

You can bomb networks.
You can arrest cells. You can pressure commanders.

But unless you understand the belief system that fuels it, you will keep treating symptoms.

That is why the puzzle stays unsolved. So I am not denying geopolitics. Or manipulation.
Or intelligence games. I am saying this:
If we reduce ISIS to a puppet, we stop studying the thing that actually reproduces it.
And then we stay surprised when it comes back.

Hello, I think you are onto a nugget of truth with this lecture but your coverage of personality archetypes and psychology leaves much to be desired or corrected. To start psychopaths are simply people who are unable to empathize with others, they are unable to feel compassion and understand social human emotions. People with multiple personalities disorder are a completely different classification it is not psychopathy, they have multiple personalities within that are all capable of feeling empathy. People with psychopathy commonly engage in what we call 'masking' where they form a persona fit for each scenario, they have no understanding of the emotional state of the other party so there are only optimal moves that have better outcomes. It's also common among autistic people but for different reasons and with vastly different results. Psychopaths seem like different people depending on the audience because they want something different from each audience, this should not be misconstrued as multiple personality disorder.

### **@willowbeederouaux4750** | 👍 3 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6PdfD44DkqA&lc=UgwBsneZCqOe6X1YQS14AaABAg)

As someone who actually lives with a dissociative disorder (OSDD), I need to speak up, b/c this part of the lecture is not just inaccurate, it’s harmful. 


Dissociation and “multiple personalities” are not signs of manipulation, deception, or psychopathy. They are the direct result of trauma (real trauma, the kind of trauma children survive because their minds had no other way to cope).


When you’ve lived through that, when you’ve dissociated to survive things no child should experience, it’s painful to hear your reality framed as something “evil” or dangerous. People with dissociative disorders are NOT predators. We’re people who endured overwhelming experiences and developed a protective system to keep going.

 (and btw, MPD is an outdated term, and the disorder is now known as DID. The professor should know this especially if he's lecturing abt this very serious mental disorder)


I understand the professor often speaks in metaphors but using real psychiatric conditions as symbols for “mind control” or “lack of empathy” spreads stigma about disorders that already carry deep misunderstanding. It makes people like me feel erased, misrepresented, or treated like a threat when the truth is the exact opposite.


I’m not asking anyone to reject the lecture, but just to be critical. Be compassionate. The psychological claims here are not aligned with clinical science, and they can hurt real people.


If anyone watching struggles with dissociation or lives with DID/OSDD, please know you are not what’s being described here. You are not a psychopath. You are a trauma survivor, and what you developed was a shield, not a weapon.
